New York, Sept. 10. 

The possibility of the introduc- 
tion of a definite currency infla- 
tion programme within the next 
three months is now envisaged. 

‘The inflation scheme In contem- 
plation, accordiuy to hints by Mr, 
Wallace, the Secretary of the Ag- 
riculture Department, provides for 
the reduction of the old content 
of the dollar. *

Wallace as saying that the people 
are likely ‘to get impatient in tho 
next three months and .to demand 
a btrong dose of inflation unless 
the disparity belween the farmers!

his purchases can be overcome by

through. pay-rolls and not into in- 
creased profits for manufacturers,

HOBBIES OF. T! 


"By DOUGLAS. WEST.’ 


HE discovery of. 


-spot on the planet 


Saturn. by Mr. ° Will iay| 


who is as much. at home 
vith telescopo trained on

have owed in every age, to 
the labours of men - wit 
hobbies. ; 


Congreve regarded hia plays 
as diversions of a man of fashion. 
Prince. Rupert, the cavalry 
Jender, dabbled in chemistry to 
the world’s profit. . Trollope 
wrote his novels before going to 
his day’s work at the Post Office. 
Lord Grey of Falleden found

the birds in his garden, and has 
recorded their habits in books of 
enduring value and chatm. © 
Mr. Churchill is a painter or a

sure.. Mr,’ H.-G. Wells used to

soldiers on batticfields designed by. 
himaolf. Mr. Myxton makes mor- 
dant sketches of his fellow mem- 
bers In the House of Commons. 
Most men of active mind dis- 
cover carly in life the need of a 


hobby im order to avoid tho) 


tedium of unoccupied hours. 
Many turn to collecting, and find 
lasting pleasure in the acquisition 
of pictures, silver, china, stamps, 
and coins, or thd. more: exotic 
debris of the world. Mr." Henry 
Ford, for instance, collects old gin 
bottles—a melancholy ‘hobby. for a 
tectotaller. : : F 

Sir Henry Wood, the conductor, 
ia a skilful amateur carpenter. 
Mr. George Robey ,is not only an 
excellent performer. on the violin 
but also makes his own violins. 
His aptitudd, may be judged from 
the fact: that Kreisler has played 
on ono of them ‘at a concert.

_ Foreign circles. {n Peking are

‘Mias'Emmotine Argucllo, private 
secrotary to Mr. Nelson T. Johnson, 
the Amorlcan Min{ster to China, 
| while riding on the bridle-path 


. + -—yournd—the-Glacis--yestorday...——. 


1 % . 

The Japanese military, without 
troubling to notify thoother Lega- 
_ tions, or oven to orect a notlee- 
board; suddenly declded to stop 
all riding on their part of the 
Glacis during millitary exercises, 
mercly handing riders a pencilled’ 
and unsigned note to this effect. © 


Miss Arguello, ignoring the note, 
continue to ride, but, on returning, 
she was surrrounded by‘a dozen 
soldiers with fixed bayonets. One 
of the soldiers threw a large stone 
at the pony; others struck her on 
the arm, snatched away her riding 
cane and broke it. Others selzed 

' the pony’s bridle and tried to dra 
Miss Arguello awny. a 


Miss Argucllo was saved from 
further violence by the intervention 
of . Commandant —_Benoit-Guyod, 
Commandant/of the French Lega- 
tion Guard, who went to her assist- 
ance.

OVESEAS VICTORIES. 

This fs the second time in the 
history of the American champion- 
ship that the title has been }von 
by an Englishman, and the first 
time since 1928 that it has fallen 
to an overseas player. 

H. L. Doherty, one of the most 
famous tennis players the world 
has ever known was the first 
Englishman successfully to visit 
America in quest of the champion- 
ship. f 

He won it in 1903, It was not 
until 23 years later that the title 
again iwent out of the country. 
Then Rene Lacoste, the Frenchman 
captured ‘it and he retained it the 
following year. In 1928 Henri 
Cochet won tho title for the: first 
and only time.

NEXT. WEEK'S

(By “Veritaa”) 


By their victory against Crai- 
gengower on Saturday, the 
Kowloon Bowling Green Club 
not only kept alive their in- 
terest in the destination of the 
accond division championship 
of the bowls league, but made 
certain of at lenst one thing— 
whatever. else happens they 
must finish runners-up. 


It cannot be said that Craigen- 
gower gave a display worthy ‘of 
champions. They were, decisively 
beaten on all three rinks. On the 
other hand the Botvling Green 
played their best bowls of the sea- 
son, excellent team work being 
noticeable. 


Civil Service's prospecta of kéep- 
ing in the ruining were finally 
ruined by the Police ‘ who/TDeat 
them with surprising ease, being 
ahead on.all rinks. 


The position is now clear. Crai- 
gengower must win against Civil 
Service next Saturday if they are 
to retain the championship. Their 
defeat would mean a deciding 
‘match with the Bowling Green 
Club, thus opening up the pros- 
pects of the Peninsula club achie- 
ving the “double.”

REMEDIOS BROTHERS. LEAVE 
FOR MACAO ., 


* Shanghai, Sept. 9. 

Patricio dos Remedios, the self- 
confessed murderer of the hup- 
less Choy Ling, and his brother, 
Jose dos Remedios, have left for 

Hongkong aboard the Messageries 
Maritimes Hner, Felix Roussel. 

Thoy «will be tuken to Macno to 
stand their trinl for the murder. 

The murder was.committed on 
the night of August 4, and was 
one of the most brutal in the his- 
tory of Shanghai crime. Accord- 
ing to n confession made by Patri- 
clo, he murdered Choy Ling w ith 
un {ce breaker. Later, ho packed 
her body fn a black trunk and sent 
it to Kobe, aban the Shanghai 
Maru.. 

When no one came to claim the 
trunk, and the man to whom it was 
addressed could not be found, cus- 
toms officinlls forced it open and 
made a gruesome discovery. 

A Russian. and-a Japanese were 
suspected of the érime before tho 
trail of evidence led to the Remc- 
dios Brothers. Seon after it was 
established that Choy Ling was 
Patricio’ common law. wife, the 
brothera were arrested. 

in omaking. his 
Patricio clenred his brother of 
any complicity in the crime. THe 
snid Jose knew nothing of it until

hat Maru.

CAR TURNS OVER AT 
CASTLE PEAK 


Four ocerpants of a pr ivate car 
had an alarming experience tvhen 
the Morria Sedan in which. they 
were travelling: skidded and turn- 
ed over-on its side on the Castle 
Peak Road about 2.30 p.m. yester- 
day. 

The car belonged to Mr. Cheung 
Yuk-sai, and was being driven. by 
hia private driver, Lau Hing. Two 
cther friends were in the car at the 
which -took 
place near, Ping Shan. ‘Except, for 
n severe shaking, the occupants of 
the car did not receive any serious 
injuries. 

Efforts were made to put tho car 
back on to the road again, but this 
proving futile the party returned to 
Kowloon, and the abandcned car 
was left-to be righted-laten -Thé 
car wag but’ slightly ‘damaged, ‘but 
all the windows were broken.

me Hollywood, California. 
et by the latest outbreak of 


kidnapping In. the U.S.A. Holly- 
wood igs redoubling its precautions 


‘Lagainst kidnappers. ‘. 


Fear of kidnapping, alwaya pre- 
sent among the colony’s 20,000 
actors _and actresses, has been 


hoightoned by recont abductions in’ 


other parts of the country. 
Leading’ studios already"guarded 
by private police, are discussing 
formation of a “Scotland Yard” to 
protect their: film stars and direc- 
tors, nnd Los Angeles county and 


.Jeity officials are taking precaution- 


ary steps. , 2 ; 
The loca! sheriff has a group of 
secret investigators to handle kid- 
napping cases, and has warned 
wealthy fantilies to bo on their 
guard. | Pee 
The Chief of Police has ordered 
his men to keep a finger on boot- 
loggers who have been forced out 
of business by repeal of liqour 
Jaws, and has issued orders to shoot 
to kill in combatting kidnappers. 
 Hollywood’s concern {gs not one 
of idle apprehension. Two men 
convicted, several years ago of cun- 
triving a plot to abduct one of the 
most famous of ‘its film celebrities, 
Mary Pickford, aro serving sen- 
tences of from 10 to 50 years. 
Marlene Dietrich, the German 
actress now ih Europe, had bars 
put on her Beverly Hills home, and 
later hired guards for her young 
daughter 24 hours a day. An armed 
guard accompanied them to Europe. 
PROTECTING CHILDREN. 
Ann Hafding has a special guard 
for her daughter, June Bannister.' 
Harold Lloyd, the millionaire, 
bespectacled funster whose estate 
fs a.showplice, has a guard for his 
three children. So has Gloria 
Swanson. : : 
One studio already is perfecting 
a detective organization in its pri- 
vate police system to keep an ear 
close to underworld movements 
which might bode evil for its stars 
and officials. rr, 
So marked has become__the ap- 
Neiston of film personnges they 
are accompanied by bodyguards 
even when they attend theatre “pre- 
miers” (first nights) and other 
public functions, When on star 
travels, studio ,officials prequently 
arrange with police departments en 
route to afford them special pro- 
tection.—Reuter. :

OF MR. GIBSON 


Washington. - 

When Mr. Hugh Gibson, general- 
ly rated one of the most experi- 
enced American © diplomats was 
shifted from Brussels to Brazil py 
President Roosevelt in his: admini- 
stration, it struck some Washing- 
ton observers as odd. 

‘Mr, Gibson's years of experience 
with the problem of disarmament, 
at a time when it seomed that tha 
Roosevelt administration. was set- 
ting out. with high hopes to get 
somewhere in tho ‘disarmament 
field, appeared indispensable. 

But Diplomatic and trade circles 
in Washington aro looking upon 
Mr. Gibson’s appointment to Rio 
de Janeiro as a significant move 
in the Roosevelt administrations 
intention to do all possible in re- 
capturing for the United States’ 
export trade the once lucrative 


«|markets of South America. 


Since he {8 well schooled in the 
statecraft and trade practices of 
this country’s commercial rivals, 
the possibility of concluding with 
Brazil in agreement for reciprocal 
Itrade favours to build up com- 
merce between tho two grent na- 
tional entities of North and South 
America may, be Mr. Gibson's mis- 
sion at Rio.——Reuter. » :

‘OTHER INSPIRATION. - 


-In addition to“ the “ghostly. In- |. 
fluences” of these three mon, tho |. 


defondants all claimed ‘to havo re- 
eclved inspiration from studying 
the Mves and actions of those who 
brought about tho Takiwa Roforms 
in the 7th Contury and the Meiji 


Restoration 60 years ago,-actions |~ 


which, though they brought great 
good, were frequently both violent 
and ruthleas in execution. 


The Great Saigo, the leador of 
the Satsuma rebellion of 1877, was 
alago quoted as providing: inspira- 
tion for the intended coup d'etat, 
for Saigo, Uke themselves, rose 
not against the Emperor, to whom 
ho was passionately devoted, but 
ugainst the Government, which he 
considered to be acting contrary 
i the true interests of the coun- 
ry. 


Tribute was also paid by the de. 
fendants to the assassins of the 
Regent, Kamon no Kami, who was 
slain In 1860 by reactionary 


“patriots” because his overtures |. 


to the foreign Powers were view- 
ed with alarm, as contrary to 
Japan's interests, , a 


Testimony was also given to the 
influence exerted by the deeds 
of the 47’ Ronin who, in 1703, 
avenged the death of their master 
by slaying the lord who had caua- 
ed his death and then, in turn, 
committed hara-kiri, 


LENIENCY EXPLAINED. 


‘From these and other deeds 
have arisen that curious code 
which permits the Japanese: as a 
nation to view with such extra- 
ordinary calmness and leniency 
the actions of those who commit 
acts of violence in the name of 
loyalty. and patriotism, provided 
the perpetrators are prepared to 
prove their sincerity of purpose 
either by subsequent self-immola- 
tion or by voluntary surrender to 
the authorities. 


This code Has been strictly fol- 
lowed by the present defendants, 
‘for, having committed their acts 
of violence on May 15, last year, 
they surrendered In a body to the 
gendarmerie authorities for 
tunishment and, during the court- 
martials, have petitioned for capi- 
tal punishment for themselves, 


it is significant that two of the 
cadets involved testified to having 
visited the zraves of the 47 Ronin 
on'the morning of May 15 in order 
to puiy their respects to the spirits 
of those whose actions of 230 years 
ago they themselves were about 
to emulate.—Renter.

ARGENTINE NOTE TO 
UNITED STATES — 


ee ‘ ' WASHINGTON, SEPT: 10. 
THIRTY-NINE AMERICAN WARSHIPS ARE 
NOW CONCENTRATED IN CUBAN WATERS OR 
WITHIN A FEW HOURS OF HAVANA, AND WITH 
THE NEW REVOLUTIONARY REGIME UNDER 
DR. RAMON GRAU SAN MARTIN STRUGGLING 
AGAINST ODDS TO ASSERT ITS AUTHORITY, A 
HIGHLY TENSE SITUATION EXISTS. . ; 

Perfect order reigns for the moment, but outlook 

is causing apprehension, owing to the paradoxical situa- 

tion in the Army in which the new government proposes 

that the highest rank shall be that of Colonel and that 

the sergeants shall clicose the’ officers. Those chosen, 
however, refuse to serve and stand by Dr. Cespedes. 

Talk of intervention by the United States continues, 

but feeling in South America is illustrated in a Note 

handed by the Argentine Government to the American 

Ambassador, welcoming President Roosevelt’s ardent 
desire not to intervene in Cuba and pointing out that 
youthful nations can work out their own destiny if they 
are left to.their own'devices. The New York Herald’s 
Washington correspondent interprets the Note as strong 
‘advice not-to intervene in any circumstances and looking 
askance at the U.S. naval array in Cuban waters.

Virginia, and Commander Williams,

Regiment which. has been 
mobilised there for possible duty 
In Cuba.

immediate. movement and should 
a2 call come, many of them. will 
be transported to Cuba by’ acro-

dent Roosevelt resumed the yacht 
cruise which was cancelled yester-

“OF MACAO 
Arrangements for Big 

Reception 


: Macao, Sept. 11. 

Arrangements for receiving His 
Excellency Licut, Col. Bernardes de 
Miranda at Macao, on his return 
from Portugal, are proceeding, and 
tho contribution of the Chinese 
community to the celebrations Is 
to be a very big one, with decora- 
tions along.the principal strects, 
and crackers and fireworks | at 
several points. During the night 
there will be electric iluminations 
as well. : 


The Governor will reach Macao 
at‘’6.00 p.m. on Wednesday and 
will be accorded a public weleome 
at the Municipal Hall. 


Subsequently he will receive the 
visits of members of the public 
and officials at Government House, 
and in the ovening an official 
dinner will be given in his honour. 


H. E, the Governor of Macao 
will) be received in Hongkong by 
Major Perogrino da Costa, Direc- 
tor of the Health Department, as 
the doyen of the departmental 
chiefa in Macao. 


Lieut. the Marquis de Ficalho, 
A..D;.C. to Dr. Rocha Santos, 
OMcer Administering thet Govern- 
ment of Macao; will also be pro- 
ceeding to Hongkong to receive 
Col. Miranda.

nor of Macao will be entertained 
in Hongkong by members of the 
‘Portuguese community in’ the 
British colony.—QOur Own Corres-

‘FOOTBALL 


(By “Tho Pilgrim”) | 


By their surprising defeat of 
‘Leicester City 6n the mid- 
landers own ground on Satur- 
day, Tottenham now take pre- 
mier position in the First Divi- 
sion to which they were pro- 
/moted this season. 


“This is the first match Leicester 
have lost at‘home and ft indicates 
either that the virlle defence, 
tvhich they have been at some 


pains to build up, has not fulfilled 


expectations or that the Spurs 
lender Hunt, has infused match 
winning Ifo into the fowanl Hne. 

Manchester City crented con- 
stornation among the puntera by 
holding the Arsenal to a draw on 
the Highbury ground and in 80 
doing emphasized thelr splendid 
‘team fwork, 
this season, 

WEDNESDAY'S RETORT. 

The Wednesday gave an apt 
retort to those critics who hinted 
that the club fvas in danger of 
falling below first division standard 
by getting the odd goal in five 
against Everton on- the latters 
ground. ..Birmingham  conclusive- 
ly defeated Middlesboro’—another 
away match—and Derby journeyed. 
to Newcastle to share points with 
the Cup holders. : 
‘ The Wanderers failed dismally 
before their own supporters 
conceding six gonls to Sunder- 
land. The Jatter are one of 
the youngest and best balansed 
sides in League football and are 
due for a bright season, whilst 
on the other hand: the Wolves are 
taking serious risks with -their 
defence. Both clubs are gambl- 
ing with young players but with 
very different results. 

STRUGGLING UNITED. 

In the Second Division Preston 
and Notts County lost their un- 
beaten records in away * en- 
counters, Grimsby accounting 
easily. for the. former and South- 
ampton taking the points by an 
odd goal against the County. 

The United are still struggling 
“and that js all thatican be said. 
Burnley did well to wrest points 
‘from tha Hammers who gave them 
very reluctintly at Upton Park. 

Crystal Palace. visited Reading 
and satisfied their supporters by 
a goalless draw. Newport fell 
before Aldershot, registering their 
second: home ‘loss.: There were 
few surprises in the North section 
of the Third Division though New 
Brighton's profitable journey to 
Carlisle was not an assured thing. 

Scottish football upset many 
coupons, Partick taking away 2 
point from Clyde and Dundee tak- 
ing both from Aberdeen. 
and Rangers divided the honours 
whilst the luckless Queen O’South 
fell again on ‘their own ground, 
this time to Ayr United.

NEXT * WEEK’S 
“VITAL GAME 


(By “Veritas”) , 


By their victory against Crai- 
géngower on Saturday, the 
Kowloon Bowling Green Club 
not only kept alive their in-. 
terest in the destination of the 
second division championship 
of the bowls Icaguc, but made 
certain of ot least one thing— 
whatever clse happens they 
must finish runners-up. 


It cannot be anid that Craigen- 
gower give a display worthy of 
champions. They were decisively 
beaten on all three rinks. On the 
other -hand the Bowling Green 
played their best bowls of the sea- 
son, excellent team work | being 
noticeable, 


Civil Service's prospects of keep- 
ing in the running were finally 
ruined by .the. Police who beat 
them with surprising case, being 
ahead.on all rinks. 


The position is now clear. Crai- 
gongower must win against Civil 
Service. next Saturday if they are 
to retain the championship. Their 
defeat would mean a deciding 
match with the Bowling Green 
Club, thus opening up ‘the pros- 
pects of the Peninsula club achie- 
ving the “double.” 


INTERPORTERS ON VIEW. 


The practice matches between 
the Interport rinks wore somewhat 
disappointing. Very few of the 
players maintained usual .form. 
The ‘real excoption was Raoul Luz, 
who, against what is gen- 
erally regarded. os the strongest 
of the Interport rinks, played in- 
spired bowls, and led his man toa 
28-7 victory. 

The players appeared - to. bo 
“affected by the knowledge that 
they were more or less_on_ trial 


and in endeavouring to put thelr 
best,into the game, became over- 
anxious with unfortunate results. 
‘However, the matches had the 
benefit of allowing the ‘rinks to 
develop tenm work and further 
practice games will do much to-| 
wards cementing them into a 
strong team, :

material for the football classics. 
Tho northern and southern dis- 
tricts of this country will also be 
represented,

UNITED STATES.

been able to do during the Jast twelve months—he beat 
Jack Crawford, holder of the Australian, French and 
Wimbledon titles, in the final of a national champion- 
ship by 6-3, 11-13, 4-6, 6-0, 6-1. -

' world’s record in lawn tennis. Crawford required only to win 
to-day to secure all four’ major tennis titles of the world.

' career that Perry has won a national singles title. :

ON PEACE MISSION 


: Capetown, Sept. 11. 
_Viee-Admiral FE. R. G. R. 
Evans (“Evans of the Broke’) 
the Commander-in-Chief of the 
Africn .Station, who~ is acting 
High Commissioner in’ Bechu- 
analand, is leaving Simonstown 
to-day: for Bechuanaland. 

Ho ‘will be accompanied by 
two hundred. marines and three 
howitzers. - 

Vice-Admiral vans. is travel- 
ling filand to investigate a report 
from the. Magistrate at Serowe 
‘that a European resident there has 
been: flogged by sentence of the 
Kyotln native court... 

He will also arbitrate 
dispute between the native and 
the European: residents of the 
disturbed area. 38 

It is stated that the Bechuana- 
iInnd natives are peacefully dis- 
posed and it is therefore presumed 
that the despatch of the marings 
is a precautionary measure, nl- 
though it is significant thit the 
flagship, T1.M.S. Carlisle hastened 
from Durban to Simonstown a day 
carly, enabling Admiral Evans and 
his escort to Jeave with a minimum 
of delay.

has probably had more adventures

known not only in the Navy, but 
algo to thousands of people who 
remember how in April, 1917,
